\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{i})
, or equivalently, the
number of
p<\frac{\ell}{2}
such that
\operatorname{Bit}(2p,n)=1
and

\operatorname{Bit}(2p+1,n)=1
Using the formula
\textsf{seq}(y)
and the bit
predicate, we can easily express this by a term
\textsf{seqlen}(y)
.

To prove (3), we first write an arithmetical formula

\textsf{isEntry}(y,y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})
such that

\left\lsem\textsf{isEntry}\right\rsem(j,n,i)=1\iff n=\ullcorner(i_{0},\ldots,i_{k-1})\ulrcorner,j<k,i=i_{j}\text{ for some
}k,i_{0},\ldots,i_{k-1}\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Once we have this formula, we let

\textsf{entry}(y,y^{\prime})=\textsf{min}\Big(y^{\prime},\#z<y^{\prime}.\exists y^{\prime\prime}<y^{\prime}.\big(\textsf{isEntry}(y,y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\wedge z<y^{\prime\prime}\big)\Big).

To define
\textsf{isEntry}(y,y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})
, observe that for
\boldsymbol{i}=(i_{0},\ldots,i_{k-1})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
, the
j
th entry
i_{j}
is located
between the
j
th and
(j+1)
st ’
\#
’ in the string
\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{i})

and thus between the
j
th and
(j+1)
st occurrence of ’
11
’ at
positions
2p,2+1
in the string
\sigma_{2}(\boldsymbol{i})=\bin(\ullcorner\boldsymbol{i}\ulrcorner)
. Using the bit predicate, we can thus extract the bit
representation of
i_{j}
from
\ullcorner\boldsymbol{i}\ulrcorner
in
FO+C
.

Lemma 3.18
.

There are arithmetical
FO+C
-terms
\textsf{flog}(y)
and

\textsf{clog}(y)
such that for all for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
,

\left\lsem\textsf{flog}\right\rsem(n)=\left\lfloor\log n\right\rfloor\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lsem\textsf{clog}\right\rsem(n)=\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil.

Proof 3.19
.

This is straightforward, observing that
\left\lfloor\log n\right\rfloor
is the
highest
1
-bit in the binary representation of
n
.

Proof 3.20

(Proof of Lemma
3.14
)
.

For the proof, it will be convenient to fix a numerical assignment

\mathcal{a}
. Of course the
FO+C
-expressions we shall define will not
depend on
\mathcal{a}
. Let
m\coloneqq\mathcal{a}(U)
, and for
0\leq i<m
, let

n_{i}\coloneqq\left\llangle Y,U\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}(i)
. Then
n_{i}<2^{m}
and, and for
0\leq j<m
we have

\operatorname{Bit}(j,n_{i})=1\iff(j,i)\in\mathcal{a}(Y).

It is our goal to compute
\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}n_{i}
in such a way that
the computation can be expressed in
FO+C
. More precisely, we want
to define a formula
s-itadd

and a term

bd-s-itadd
, which both may use the variables
Y
and

U
, such that

\left\llangle\textsf{s-itadd},\textsf{{bd-s-itadd}}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}n_{i}.

Since

\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}n_{i}<2^{2m},

we can simply let
\textsf{bd-s-itadd}\coloneqq 2U
. Thus we only
need to define the formula
\textsf{s-itadd}(\widehat{y})
in such a way
that for all
j<2m
we have

\left\lsem\textsf{s-itadd}\right\rsem^{\mathcal{a}}(j)=\operatorname{Bit}\left(j,\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}n_{i}\right).

(6)

Without of loss of generality we may assume that
m
is
sufficiently large, larger than some absolute constant that can be
extracted from the proof. If
m
is smaller than this constant,
we simply compute the sum by repeatedly applying
Lemma
3.12
(1).

Our construction will be inductive, repeatedly transforming the
initial sequence of numbers
n_{i}
into new sequences that have the
same sum. It will be convenient to use the index
(0)
for the
initial family. Thus we let

m^{(0)}\coloneqq m
, and
n^{(0)}_{i}\coloneqq n_{i}
,
for all

i\in\{0,\ldots,m^{(0)}-1\}
. Furthermore, it will be useful to
let
\ell^{(0)}\coloneqq m^{(0)}
, because at later stages
t
of the
construction the size
m^{(t)}
of the current family of numbers will
no longer be identical with their bitlength
\ell^{(t)}
.

For
j<\ell^{(0)}
, let

n^{(0)}_{i,j}\coloneqq\operatorname{Bit}(j,n^{(0)}_{i})
and

s^{(0)}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(0)}-1}n^{(0)}_{i,j}=\big|\big\{i\mathrel{\big|}n^{(0)}_{i,j}=1\big\}\big|.

(7)

Then

\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(0)}-1}n^{(0)}_{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(0)}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{\ell^{(0)}-1}2^{j}n^{(0)}_{i,j}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell^{(0)}-1}2^{j}\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(0)}-1}n^{(0)}_{i,j}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell^{(0)}-1}2^{j}s^{(0)}_{j}.

Let
m^{(1)}\coloneqq\ell^{(0)}
and
n^{(1)}_{i}\coloneqq 2^{i}s^{(0)}_{i}

for
i<\ell^{(0)}
. Then

\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}n_{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(0)}-1}n_{i}^{(0)}=\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(1)}-1}n_{i}^{(1)}.

Moreover,

n^{(1)}_{i}\leq 2^{\ell^{(0)}-1}s^{(0)}_{i}=2^{\ell^{(0)}-1+\log s^{(0)}_{i}}<2^{\ell^{(0)}+\left\lfloor\log s^{(0)}_{i}\right\rfloor}.

Let
p^{(1)}\coloneqq\left\lfloor\log m^{(0)}\right\rfloor
and
\ell^{(1)}\coloneqq\ell^{(0)}+p^{(1)}
. Noting that
s^{(0)}_{i}\leq m^{(0)}

for all
i
,
we thus have

n^{(1)}_{i}<2^{\ell^{(1)}}

for all
i
. Finally, note that

\operatorname{Bit}(j,n^{(1)}_{i})\neq 0\implies i\leq j\leq i+p^{(1)}

This completes the base step of the construction. For the inductive
step, suppose that we have defined
m^{(k)},\ell^{(k)},p^{(k)}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
and

n^{(k)}_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,m^{(k)}-1\}
such that

\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}n_{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(k)}-1}n_{i}^{(k)}

and
for all
i
:

•

n^{(k)}_{i}<2^{\ell^{(k)}}
for all
i
;

•

\operatorname{Bit}(j,n^{(k)}_{i})\neq 0\implies i\leq j\leq i+p^{(k)}
.

For
j<\ell^{(k)}
, let

n^{(k)}_{i,j}\coloneqq\operatorname{Bit}(j,n^{(k)}_{i})
and

s^{(k)}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(k)}-1}n^{(k)}_{i,j}=\sum_{i=\max\{0,j-p^{(k)}\}}^{j}n^{(k)}_{i,j}.

Then

\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(k)}-1}n^{(k)}_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell^{(k)}-1}2^{j}s^{(k)}_{j}.

Let
m^{(k+1)}\coloneqq\ell^{(k)}
and
n^{(k+1)}_{i}\coloneqq 2^{i}s^{(k)}_{i}
for
i<\ell^{(k)}
. Then

\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}n_{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(k)}-1}n_{i}^{(k)}=\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(k+1)}-1}n_{i}^{(k+1)}.

Moreover,

n^{(k+1)}_{i}\leq 2^{\ell^{(k)}-1}s^{(k)}_{i}=2^{\ell^{(k)}-1+\log s^{(k)}_{i}}<2^{\ell^{(k)}+\left\lfloor\log s^{(k)}_{i}\right\rfloor}.

Note that
s^{(k)}_{i}\leq p^{(k)}+1
. Let
p^{(k+1)}\coloneqq\left\lfloor\log(p^{(k)}+1)\right\rfloor
and
\ell^{(k+1)}\coloneqq\ell^{(k)}+p^{(k+1)}
. Then

n^{(k+1)}_{i}<2^{\ell^{(k+1)}}

and for all
j
,

\operatorname{Bit}(j,n^{(k+1)}_{i})\neq 0\implies i\leq j\leq i+p^{(k+1)}.

Observe that if

p^{(k)}=1
then
p^{(k^{\prime})}=1
for all
k^{\prime}>k
. Let
k^{*}
be the least

k
such that
p^{(k)}=1
. It is easy to see that

\begin{array}[]{rcccll}&&p^{(k^{*}-1)}&=&2,\\
3&\leq&p^{(k^{*}-2)}&\leq&6,\\
7&\leq&p^{(k^{*}-3)}&\leq&126,\\
127&\leq&p^{(k)}&&&\text{for }k<k^{*}-3.\end{array}

(8)

{techclaim}

1.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{k^{*}}ip^{(i)}\leq 7\log\log m
;

2.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k^{*}}\bsize(p^{(i)})\leq 7\log\log m
.

{subproof}

By induction on
k=k^{*},k^{*}-1,\ldots,2
we prove

\sum_{i=k}^{k^{*}}i\cdot p^{(i)}\leq 3kp^{(k)}

(9)

As base case, we need to check (
9
) for

k\in\{k^{*},k^{*}-1,k^{*}-2,k^{*}-3\}
. Using (
8
), this is straightforward. For example, if
k=k^{*}-2
and

p^{(k)}=3
we have

\sum_{i=k}^{k^{*}}ip^{(i)}=3(k^{*}-2)+2(k^{*}-1)+k^{*}=6k^{*}-8\leq 9k^{*}-18=3kp^{(k)}.

The inequality
6k^{*}-8\leq 9k^{*}-18
holds because if
2\leq k\leq k^{*}-2
then

k^{*}\geq 4
, which implies
3k^{*}\geq 12
.
Similarly, if
k=k^{*}-3
and
p^{(k)}=15
we have

p^{(k+1)}=\left\lfloor\log(p^{(k)}+1)\right\rfloor=4
and thus

\sum_{i=k}^{k^{*}}ip^{(i)}=15(k^{*}-3)+4(k^{*}-2)+2(k^{*}-1)+k^{*}=22k^{*}-55\leq 45k^{*}-135=3kp^{(k)}.

The inequality
22k^{*}-55\leq 45k^{*}-135
holds because if
2\leq k\leq k^{*}-3
then

k^{*}\geq 5
.

For the inductive step
k+1\mapsto k
, where
2\leq k<k^{*}-3
, we argue as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=k}^{k^{*}}i\cdot p^{(i)}

\displaystyle=k\cdot p^{(k)}+\sum_{i=k+1}^{k^{*}}i\cdot p^{(i)}

\displaystyle\leq k\cdot p^{(k)}+3(k+1)p^{(k+1)}

induction hypothesis

\displaystyle\leq k\cdot p^{(k)}+3(k+1)\log(p^{(k)}+1)

\displaystyle\leq k\cdot p^{(k)}+4k\log(p^{(k)}+1)

\displaystyle\text{because }k\geq 2.

Since by (
8
) we have
p^{(k)}\geq 127
for
k<k^{*}-3
, we have
p^{(k)}\geq 4\log(p^{(k)}+1)
. Inequality (
9
) follows:

\sum_{i=k}^{k^{*}}i\cdot p^{(i)}\leq p^{(k)}+4k\log(p^{(k)}+1)\leq 2kp^{(k)}.

For
k=2
, this yields

\sum_{i=2}^{k^{*}}i\cdot p^{(i)}\leq 6p^{(2)}\leq 6\log(p^{(1)}+1)\leq 6\log(\log m+1)\leq 7\log\log m.

To prove (2), note that
\bsize(p^{(k^{*})})=\bsize(1)=1
and

\bsize(p^{(k)})=\left\lceil\log(p^{(k)}+1)\right\rceil\leq p^{(k+1)}+1
for

k<k^{*}
. Thus (2) holds if
k^{*}=1
. If
k^{*}\geq 2
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k^{*}}\bsize(p^{(i)})=1+\sum_{i=2}^{k^{*}}(p^{(i)}+1)=k^{*}+\sum_{i=2}^{k^{*}}p^{(i)}\leq k^{*}+1+\sum_{i=2}^{k^{*}-1}p^{(i)}\leq\sum_{i=2}^{k^{*}}ip^{(i)},

and (2) follows from (1).

{techclaim}

There is an arithmetical
FO+C
-term
\textsf{pseq}(y)
such
that

\left\lsem\textsf{pseq}\right\rsem^{\mathcal{a}}(m)=\ullcorner(p^{(1)},\ldots,p^{(k^{*})})\ulrcorner.

{subproof}

Let

\displaystyle\varphi(y,z)\coloneqq\,

\displaystyle\textsf{seq}(z)\wedge\textsf{entry}(0,z)=\textsf{flog}(y)

\displaystyle\wedge\forall y^{\prime}<\textsf{seqlen}(z)-1.\textsf{entry}(y^{\prime}+1,z)=\textsf{flog}(\textsf{entry}(y^{\prime},z)+1)

where the formula
seq
and the terms
entry
,

seqlen
are from Lemma
3.16
and the term

flog
is from Lemma
3.18
. Then for all

q\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

\left\lsem\varphi\right\rsem^{\mathcal{a}}(m,q)=1\iff q=\ullcorner(p^{(1)},\ldots,p^{(k^{*})})\ulrcorner.

By Claim
3.20
(2) and (
5
) we have

\ullcorner(p^{(1)},\ldots,p^{(k^{*})}\ulrcorner<m
(for sufficiently large
m
). Thus the term

\textsf{pseq}(y)\coloneqq\#y^{\prime}<y.\exists z<y.\big(\varphi(y,z)\wedge y^{\prime}<z\big).

defines
\ullcorner(p^{(1)},\ldots,p^{(k^{*})})\ulrcorner
.

Recall that for
1\leq k\leq k^{*}
and
0\leq j<\ell^{(k)}
we have

s_{j}^{(k)}=\sum_{i=\max\{0,j-p^{(k)}\}}^{j}n_{i,j}^{(k)}.

To simplify the notation, in the following, we let
n_{i}^{(k)}=0
and

s_{i}^{(k)}=0
for all
k
and
i<0
. Of course then the bits

n_{i,j}^{(k)}
are also
0
, and we can write

s_{j}^{(k)}=\sum_{i=j-p^{(k)}}^{j}n_{i,j}^{(k)}.

As
n_{i,j}^{(k)}=\operatorname{Bit}(j,n_{i}^{(k)})
and
n_{i}^{(k)}=2^{i}s_{i}^{(k-1)}
,
we thus have

\displaystyle s_{j}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=j-p^{(k)}}^{j}\operatorname{Bit}(j,2^{i}s_{i}^{(k-1)})=\sum_{i=j-p^{(k)}}^{j}\operatorname{Bit}(j-i,s_{i}^{(k-1)})

\displaystyle=\big|\big\{i\mathrel{\big|}j-p^{(k)}\leq i\leq j,\operatorname{Bit}(j-i,s_{i}^{(k-1)})=1\big\}\big|.

(10)

{techclaim}

For every
t\geq 0
there is an arithmetical
FO+C
-terms
\textsf{s}^{(t)}(y)

such that for all
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

\left\lsem\textsf{s}^{(t)}\right\rsem^{\mathcal{a}}(j)=s^{(t)}_{j}.

{subproof}

We define the terms inductively, using (
7
) for the base
step and (
10
) for the inductive step.

Note that this claim only enables us to define the numbers
s^{(k^{*})}_{j}
by
a formula that depends on
k^{*}
and hence on the input, or more
formally, the assignment
\mathcal{a}
. Claim
3.20
below will show that we can
also define the
s^{(k*)}_{j}
by a formula that is independent of the
input. (We will only use Claim
3.20
to define the
s^{(2)}_{j}
.)

{techclaim}

There is an arithmetical
FO+C
-formula
\textsf{step}(y,z)

such that for all
k\in[k^{*}]
,
j\in\{0,\ldots,\ell^{(k)}-1\}
, and

j,k,s,t\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if

t=\ullcorner(s^{(k-1)}_{j-p^{(k)}},s^{(k-1)}_{j-p^{(k)}+1},\ldots,i_{s}=s^{(k-1)}_{j})\ulrcorner

then

\left\lsem\textsf{step}\right\rsem^{\mathcal{a}}(s,t)=1\iff s=s^{k}_{j}.

{subproof}

This follows easily from (
10
).

To compute
s_{j}^{(k^{*})}
, we need to know
s_{j}^{(k^{*}-1)},\ldots,s_{j-p^{(k^{*})}}^{(k^{*}-1)}
. To compute these numbers, we need to know

s_{j}^{(k^{*}-2)},\ldots,s_{j-p^{(k^{*})}-p^{(k^{*}-1)}}^{(k^{*}-2)}
, et cetera. Thus if we want to
compute
s_{j}^{(k^{*})}
starting from values
s^{(k)}_{j^{\prime}}
, we
need to know
s_{j^{\prime}}^{(k)}
for

j-\sum_{i=k+1}^{k^{*}}p^{(i)}\leq j^{\prime}\leq j.

For
2\leq k<k^{*}
, we let

\boldsymbol{s}_{jk}\coloneqq(s^{(k)}_{j-\sum_{i=k+1}^{k^{*}}p^{(i)}},\ldots,s^{(k)}_{j}),

and we let
\boldsymbol{s}_{jk^{*}}\coloneqq(s^{(k^{*})}_{j})
. Concatenating these
sequences, we let

\boldsymbol{s}_{j}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{s}_{j2}\boldsymbol{s}_{j3}\ldots\boldsymbol{s}_{jk^{*}}.

{techclaim}

There is an arithmetical
FO+C
-formula
\textsf{all-s}(y,z)
such
that for all
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
j\in\{0,\ldots,\ell^{(k^{*})}-1\}
we have

\left\lsem\textsf{all-s}\right\rsem^{\mathcal{a}}(j,t)=1\iff t=\ullcorner\boldsymbol{s}_{j}\ulrcorner.

{subproof}

This follows from Claim
3.20
(to get the
p^{(i)}
as well as

k^{*}
), Claim
3.20
(to get the base values
s^{(2)}_{j^{\prime}}
), and Claim
3.20

(to verify the internal values of the sequence), just requiring a
bit of arithmetic on the indices.

{techclaim}

There is an arithmetical
FO+C
-term
\textsf{skstar}(y)
such
that for all
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

\left\lsem\textsf{skstar}\right\rsem^{\mathcal{a}}(j)=s^{(k^{*})}_{j}.

{subproof}

We first prove that for all
j
we have

\ullcorner\boldsymbol{s}_{j}\ulrcorner<m.

(11)

The key observation is that the length of the sequence
\boldsymbol{s}_{j}
is

|\boldsymbol{s}_{j}|=\sum_{k=2}^{k*}|\boldsymbol{s}_{jk}|=\sum_{k=2}^{k*}\left(1+\sum_{i=k+1}^{k^{*}}p^{(i)}\right)\leq\sum_{k=2}^{k*}\sum_{i=k}^{k^{*}}p^{(i)}\leq\sum_{k=2}^{k^{*}}ip^{(i)}\leq 7\log\log m,

where the last inequality holds by Claim
3.20
. Moreover, for
2\leq k\leq k^{*}
and
0\leq j<\ell^{(k)}
we have
s^{(k)}_{j}\leq p^{(k)}+1\leq p^{(2)}+1\leq\log m+1
and thus
\bsize(s^{(k)}_{j})=O(\log\log m)
. Thus
\boldsymbol{s}_{j}
is a sequence of
O(\log\log m)
numbers, each of
bitsize
O(\log\log m)
. Thus the bitsize of the sequence is

O((log\log m)^{2})
, and it follows from (
5
) that for some
constant
c
,

\ullcorner\boldsymbol{s}_{j}\ulrcorner\leq 2^{c(\log\log m)^{2}}<m,

again assuming that
m
is sufficiently large. This proves
(
11
).

We let

\textsf{skstar}(y)\coloneq\exists z<U.\Big(\textsf{all-s}(y,z)\wedge y=\textsf{entry}\big(\textsf{seqlen}(z)-1,z\big)\Big),

where the formula
\textsf{all-s}(y,z)
is from Claim
3.20
and the terms

entry
and
seqlen
are from
Lemma
3.16
.

It remains to compute
\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(k^{*})}-1}n^{(k^{*}+1)}_{i}
, where

n_{i}^{(k^{*}+1)}=2^{i}s_{i}^{(k^{*})}
. Since
s^{(k^{*})}_{j}\leq 2
, we have

\operatorname{Bit}(j,n^{(k^{*})}_{i})=0
unless
j\in\{i-1,i\}
. We split the sum into
the even and the odd entries:

\sum_{i=0}^{m^{(k^{*})}-1}n^{(k^{*}+1)}_{i}=\underbrace{\sum_{i=0}^{\left\lceil m^{(k^{*})}/2\right\rceil-1}n^{(k^{*})}_{2i}}_{\eqqcolon n^{*}_{1}}+\underbrace{\sum_{i=0}^{\left\lfloor m^{(k^{*})}/2\right\rfloor-1}n^{(k^{*})}_{2i+1}}_{\eqqcolon n^{*}_{2}}.

Since the entries in the two partial sums have no non-zero bits in
common, it is easy to define the two partial sums (of course, using
Claim
3.20
). Then we can apply Lemma
3.12
to define

n_{1}^{*}+n_{2}^{*}
.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
Y_{1},Y_{2}
be relation variables of type
{
n
}
, and let

U_{1},U_{2}
be function variables of type
\emptyset\to\texttt{n}
.
Then there are arithmetical
FO+C
-formulas
mul
,
div
, and
FO+C
-terms

bd-mul
,
bd-div
such
that for all numerical assignments
\mathcal{a}
,

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{mul},\textsf{{bd-mul}}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}

\displaystyle=\left\llangle Y_{1},U_{1}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}\cdot\left\llangle Y_{2},U_{2}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}},

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{div},\textsf{{bd-div}}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}

\displaystyle=\left\lfloor\frac{\left\llangle Y_{1},U_{1}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}}{\left\llangle Y_{2},U_{2}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}}\right\rfloor

\displaystyle\text{if }\left\llangle Y_{2},U_{2}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}\neq 0.

Proof 3.22
.

The expressibility multiplication follows easily from the
expressibility of iterated addition (Lemma
3.14
). Division
is significantly more difficult, we refer the reader to
[
15
]
.

We need be an extension of Lemma
3.14
where the
family of numbers is no longer indexed by numbers, but by arbitrary
tuples of vertices and numbers, and where the bounds on the bitsize of
the numbers in the family are not uniform (in Lemma
3.14
we use
the
0
-ary function variable
U
to provide a bound on the bitsize of
all numbers in our family). Before presenting this
extension, we consider a version of iterated addition as well as
taking the maximum and minimum of a family of numbers given directly
as values of a function instead of the binary representation that we
considered in the previous lemmas.

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
X
be a relation variable of type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{v}^{k}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
,
and let
U,V
be function variables of types

\texttt{v}^{k}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\to\texttt{n}
,
\texttt{v}^{k}\to\texttt{n}
, respectively.

1.

There is a
FO+C
-term
u-itadd
6
6

6

The ’u’ in
u-itadd

indicates that we use a unary representation here.

such that for
all structures
A
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
,

\left\lsem\textsf{u-itadd}\right\rsem^{(A,\mathcal{a})}=\sum_{(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\mathcal{a}(U)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}),

where the sum ranges over all
(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)
such that

\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with

b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(\boldsymbol{a})
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

2.

There are
FO+C
-terms
u-max
and
u-min
such
that for all structures
A
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
,

\displaystyle\left\lsem\textsf{u-max}\right\rsem^{(A,\mathcal{a})}

\displaystyle=\max_{(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\mathcal{a}(U)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}),

\displaystyle\left\lsem\textsf{u-min}\right\rsem^{(A,\mathcal{a})}

\displaystyle=\min_{(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\mathcal{a}(U)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}),

where
\max
and
\min
range over all
(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)

such that
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with

b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(\boldsymbol{a})
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

Furthermore, if
k=0
then the terms
u-itadd
,

u-max
, and
u-min
are arithmetical.

Proof 3.24
.

To simplify the notation, in the proof we assume
\ell=1
. The
generalisation to arbitrary
\ell
is straightforward.

The trick is to use adaptive bounds in our counting terms. Let
A

be a structure and
\mathcal{a}
an assignment over
A
. Let
{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq V(A)^{k}\times{\mathbb{N}}
be the set
of all all
(\boldsymbol{a},b)\in\mathcal{a}(X)
with

b<\mathcal{a}(V)(\boldsymbol{a})
. We exploit that
for all
(\boldsymbol{a},b)\in{{\mathcal{I}}}
,
\mathcal{a}(U)(\boldsymbol{a},b)
is the
number of
c\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
c<\mathcal{a}(U)(\boldsymbol{a},b)
.
This implies

\displaystyle\sum_{(\boldsymbol{a},b)}\mathcal{a}(U)(\boldsymbol{a},b)

\displaystyle=\Big|\Big\{(\boldsymbol{a},b,c)\mathrel{\Big|}(\boldsymbol{a},b)\in{{\mathcal{I}}}\text{ and }c<\mathcal{a}(U)(\boldsymbol{a},b)\Big\}\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\Big\{(\boldsymbol{a},b,c)\mathrel{\Big|}(\boldsymbol{a},b)\in\mathcal{a}(X)\text{ with }b<\mathcal{a}(V)(\boldsymbol{a})\text{ and }c<\mathcal{a}(U)(\boldsymbol{a},b)\Big\}\Big|.

Thus

\textsf{u-itadd}\coloneqq\#\big(\boldsymbol{x},y<V(\boldsymbol{x}),z<U(\boldsymbol{x},y)\big).X(\boldsymbol{x},y).

satisfies assertion (1).

Once we have this, assertion (2) is easy because maximum and
minimum are bounded from above by the sum. For example, we let

\textsf{u-max}\coloneqq\#z<\textsf{u-itadd}.\exists\boldsymbol{x}.\exists y<V(\boldsymbol{x}).\big(X(\boldsymbol{x})\wedge z<U(\boldsymbol{x},y)\big).

The following lemma is the desired generalisation of
Lemma
3.14
.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
X,Y
be relation variables of type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{v}^{k}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}

and
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{n}\texttt{v}^{k}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
, respectively, and let
U,V
be
function variables of type
\texttt{v}^{k}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\to\texttt{n}
and

\texttt{v}^{k}\to\texttt{n}
, respectively.
Then there is an
FO+C
-formula
itadd

and an
FO+C
-term

bd-itadd
such
that for all structures
A
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
,

\left\llangle\textsf{itadd},\textsf{{bd-itadd}}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}=\sum_{(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\left\llangle Y,U\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}),

where the sum ranges over all
(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)
such that
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with

b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(\boldsymbol{a})
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

If
k=0
, then the formula
itadd
and the term

bd-itadd
are arithmetical.

Proof 3.26
.

Let
A
be a structure and
\mathcal{a}
an assignment over
A
. Let

{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq V(A)^{k}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
be the set of all all

(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)
such that
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})

with
b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(\boldsymbol{a})
. Let
m\coloneqq|{\mathcal{I}}|
and

p\coloneqq\max\big\{\mathcal{a}(U)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\mathrel{\big|}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\in{\mathcal{I}}\big\}
. Note that
p=\left\lsem\textsf{u-max}\right\rsem^{(A,\mathcal{a})}
for
the term
u-max
of Lemma
3.23
. We have to add
the family of
m
numbers

{n}_{\boldsymbol{c}}\coloneqq\left\llangle Y,U\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{c})
for

\boldsymbol{c}\in{\mathcal{I}}
. We think of these numbers as
p
-bit numbers,
padding them with zeroes if necessary. Note that we cannot directly
apply Lemma
3.14
to add these numbers, because the family,
being indexed by vertices, is not ordered, and Lemma
3.14

only applies to ordered families indexed by numbers. But there is a
simple trick to circumvent this difficulty. (We applied the same
trick in the proof of Lemma
3.14
).

For all
i<p
, we let

{n}_{{\boldsymbol{c}},i}\coloneqq\operatorname{Bit}(i,{n}_{\boldsymbol{c}})
be the
i
th bit
of
{n}_{\boldsymbol{c}}
, and we let

s_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{{\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\mathcal{I}}}{n}_{{\boldsymbol{c}},i}.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\mathcal{I}}}{n}_{\boldsymbol{c}}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\mathcal{I}}}\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}{n}_{{\boldsymbol{c}},i}\cdot 2^{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}s_{i}\cdot 2^{i}.

This reduces the problem of adding the
unordered
family of

m

p
-bit numbers
{n}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}
to adding the
ordered
family
of
p

p+\log m
-bit numbers
{n}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq s_{i}\cdot 2^{i}
.

The partial sums
s_{i}
are definable by a counting term in
FO+C
, because

s_{i}
is the number of
{\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\mathcal{I}}
such that
{n}_{{\boldsymbol{c}},i}=1
. As the bit predicate is definable in
FO+C
, we can
obtain the bit-representation of these numbers and then shift
s_{i}
by
i
to obtain the bit
representation of
{n}_{i}^{\prime}=s_{i}\cdot 2^{i}
. Then we can apply
Lemma
3.14
to compute the sum.

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
X,Y
be relation variables of type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{v}^{k}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}

and
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{n}\texttt{v}^{k}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
, respectively, and let
U,V
be
function variables of type
\texttt{v}^{k}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\to\texttt{n}
and

\texttt{v}^{k}\to\texttt{n}
, respectively.
Then there are
FO+C
-formulas
itmax
,
itmin

and
FO+C
-terms

bd-itmax
,
bd-itmin
such
that for all structures
A
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
,

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{itmax},\textsf{bd-itmax}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}

\displaystyle=\max_{(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\left\llangle Y,U\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}),

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{itmin},\textsf{bd-itmin}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}

\displaystyle=\min_{(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\left\llangle Y,U\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}),

where max and min range over all
(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)
such that
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with

b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(\boldsymbol{a})
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

If
k=0
, then the formula
itmax
,
itmin
and the term

bd-itmin
,
bd-itmin
are arithmetical.

Proof 3.28
.

Again, to reduce the notational overhead we assume
\ell=1
. We
only give the proof for the maximum; the proof for the minimum is
completely analogous.

The following formula says that
(\boldsymbol{x},y)
is the index of the
maximum number in the family:

\textsf{maxind}(\boldsymbol{x},y)\coloneqq X(\boldsymbol{x},y)\wedge y<V(\boldsymbol{x})\wedge\forall\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\forall y^{\prime}<V(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}).\big(X(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\to\textsf{leq}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x},y)\big),

where
\textsf{leq}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
is the formula
obtained from the formula
leq
of Lemma
3.12
(2)
by substituting
Y_{1}(z)
with
Y(z,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime})
,
U_{1}()
with

U(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime})
,
Y_{2}(z)
with
Y(z,\boldsymbol{x},y)
,
U_{2}()
with

U(\boldsymbol{x},y)
.

Then we let

\displaystyle\textsf{itmax}(\widehat{y})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\exists\boldsymbol{x}\exists y<V(\boldsymbol{x}).\big(\textsf{maxind}(\boldsymbol{x},y)\wedge Y(\widehat{y},\boldsymbol{x},y)\big),

bd-itmax

\displaystyle\coloneqq\#z<\textsf{u-max}.\forall\boldsymbol{x}\forall y<V(\boldsymbol{x})\big(\textsf{maxind}(\boldsymbol{x},y)\to z<U(\boldsymbol{x},y)\big),

where
u-max
is the formula of Lemma
3.23
.

3.5
Rational Arithmetic

We need to lift the results of the previous section to arithmetic on
rational numbers. However, we will run into a problem with iterated
addition, because the denominator of the sum can get too large. To
avoid this problem, we will work with arithmetic on dyadic
rationals. Then we have a problem with division, because the dyadic
rationals are not closed under division, but division is not as
important for us as iterated addition.

Our representation system for dyadic rationals by relation and
function variables, or by formulas and terms, is based on a
representations of dyadic rationals by tuples

(r,I,s,t)\in\{0,1\}\times 2^{{\mathbb{N}}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
: such a
tuple represents the number

\left\llangle r,I,s,t\right\rrangle\coloneqq(-1)^{r}\cdot 2^{-s}\cdot\sum_{i\in I,i<t}2^{i}.

This representation is not unique: there are distinct tuples
(r,I,s,t)

and
(r^{\prime},I^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
representing the same number. For
example,
\left\llangle r,I,s,t\right\rrangle=\left\llangle r,I^{\prime},s+1,t+1\right\rrangle
, where
I^{\prime}=\{i+1\mid i\in I,i<t\}
. However, each dyadic rational
q
has a unique
representation
\crep(q)=(r,I,s,t)
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

i<t
for all
i\in I
;

(ii)

s=0
or
0\in I
(that is, the fraction
\frac{\sum_{i\in I,i<t}2^{i}}{2^{s}}
is reduced);

(iii)

if
I=\emptyset
(and hence
\left\llangle s,I,s,t\right\rrangle=0
) then
r=s=t=0
.

We call
\operatorname{crep}(q)
the
canonical representation

of
q
.

To represent a dyadic rational in our logical framework, we thus need
four variables. As this tends to get a bit unwieldy, we
introduce some shortcuts.
An
r-schema
of type
{\boldsymbol{t}\to\texttt{r}}
for some

\boldsymbol{t}\in\{\texttt{v},\texttt{n}\}^{k}
is a tuple
\boldsymbol{Z}=(Z_{\textup{sg}},Z_{\textup{Ind}},Z_{\textup{dn}},Z_{\textup{bd}})
, where
Z_{\textup{sg}}
is
a relation variable of type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\boldsymbol{t}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
,
Z_{\textup{Ind}}
is a relation
variable of type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{n}\boldsymbol{t}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
, and
Z_{\textup{dn}},Z_{\textup{bd}}
are function
variables of type
\boldsymbol{t}\to\texttt{n}
.
For a structure
A
, an interpretation
\mathcal{a}
over
A
, and a tuple

\boldsymbol{c}\in A^{\boldsymbol{t}}
we
let

\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{c})\coloneqq(-1)^{r}\cdot 2^{-\mathcal{a}(Z_{\textup{dn}})(\boldsymbol{c})}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,\boldsymbol{c})\in\mathcal{a}(Z_{\textup{Ind}}),\\
i<\mathcal{a}(Z_{\textup{bd}})(\boldsymbol{c})\end{subarray}}2^{i},

(12)

where
r=1
if
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathcal{a}(Z_{\textup{sg}})
and
r=0
otherwise. Note that
with

I=\{i\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid(i,\boldsymbol{c})\in\mathcal{a}(Z_{\textup{Ind}})\}
,
s=\mathcal{a}(Z_{\textup{dn}})(\boldsymbol{c})
, and

t=\mathcal{a}(Z_{\textup{bd}})(\boldsymbol{c})
we have
\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{c})=\left\llangle r,I,s,t\right\rrangle
.

An
r-expression
is a tuple

\boldsymbol{\rho}(\boldsymbol{z})=\big(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{sg}}(\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{Ind}}(\widehat{y},\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{dn}}(\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{bd}}(\boldsymbol{z})\big)
where
\boldsymbol{z}
is a tuple of individual
variables,
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{sg}}(\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{Ind}}(\widehat{y},\boldsymbol{z})
are
FO+C
-formulas, and

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{dn}}(\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{bd}}(\boldsymbol{z})
are
FO+C
-terms. For a structure
A
, an
interpretation
\mathcal{a}
over
A
, and a tuple
\boldsymbol{c}\in A^{\tp(\boldsymbol{z})}

we let

\left\llangle\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{c})\coloneqq\left\llangle r,I,s,t\right\rrangle,

(13)

where
r=1
if
(A,\mathcal{a})\models\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{sg}}(\boldsymbol{c})
and
r=0
otherwise,
I

is the set of all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
A\models\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{Ind}}(i,\boldsymbol{c})
,

s=\left\lsem\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{dn}}\right\rsem^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{c})
, and
t=\left\lsem\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\textup{bd}}\right\rsem^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{c})
. We sometimes say that
\boldsymbol{\rho}

defines
the representation

(r,I,s,t)
of the dyadic rational
\left\llangle r,I,s,t\right\rrangle

in

(A,\mathcal{a})
.

For a fragment
L
of

FO+C
, such as those introduced in Section
3.7
,
an
r-expression in
L
is an r-expression consisting of formulas and
terms from
L
. An

arithmetical r-expression
is an r-expression consisting of
arithmetical formulas and terms.

For an r-schema
\boldsymbol{Z}
of type
{\texttt{n}^{k}\to\texttt{r}}
, for some
k\geq 0
,
and a numerical assignment
\mathcal{a}
we may just write

\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}(\boldsymbol{c})
without referring to a
structure. Similarly, for an arithmetical r-expression
\boldsymbol{\rho}(\boldsymbol{z})

we may write
\left\llangle\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}(\boldsymbol{c})
. We use a similar notation
for other objects, in particular the L,F-schemas and L,F-expressions
that will be introduced in Section
3.6
.

Lemma 3.29
.

Let
\boldsymbol{Z}
be an r-schema of type
{\emptyset\to\texttt{r}}
. Then there is an
arithmetical r-expression
crep
such that for all
structures
A
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
,
crep

defines the canonical representation of
\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}
in
(A,\mathcal{a})
.

Proof 3.30
.

Straightforward.

Using this lemma, in the following we can always assume that the
formulas and terms defining arithmetical operations, as for example,
in Lemma
3.31
,
3.33
, et cetera, return their results
in canonical representation.

Lemma 3.31
.

Let
\boldsymbol{Z}_{1},\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}
be r-schemas of type
{\emptyset\to\texttt{r}}
.

1.

There are arithmetical r-expressions
add
,

sub
, and
mul

such
that for all structures
A
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
,

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{add}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}

\displaystyle=\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}+\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})},

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{sub}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}

\displaystyle=\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}-\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})},

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{mul}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}

\displaystyle=\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}\cdot\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}.

2.

There is an arithmetical
FO+C
-formula
leq
such
that for all structures
A
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
,

(A,\mathcal{a})\models\textsf{leq}\iff\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}\leq\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}.

Proof 3.32
.

These are straightforward consequences of Lemmas
3.12
and
3.21
.

Lemma 3.33
.

Let
\boldsymbol{Z}
be an r-schema of type

{\texttt{v}^{k}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\to\texttt{r}}
. Furthermore, let
X
be a relation variable of
type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{v}^{k}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
, and let
V
be function variable of
type
\texttt{v}^{k}\to\texttt{n}
.

1.

There is an r-expression
it-add

such that for all structures
A
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
,

\left\llangle\textsf{itadd}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}=\sum_{(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}),

where the sum ranges over all
(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)
such that
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with

b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(\boldsymbol{a})
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

2.

There are r-expressions
max
and
min

such that for all structures
A
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
,

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{max}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}

\displaystyle=\max_{(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}),

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{min}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}

\displaystyle=\min_{(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}),

where
\max
and
\min
range over all
(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)

such that
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with

b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(\boldsymbol{a})
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

Furthermore, if
k=0
, then the r-expressions
itadd
,

max
, and
min
are arithmetical.

Proof 3.34
.

To express iterated addition, we first split the family of numbers
into the positive and negative numbers. We take the sums over
these two subfamilies separately and then combine the results
using Lemma
3.31
. To take the sum over a family of
nonnegative dyadic rationals, we apply Lemma
3.25
for the
numerator and Lemma
3.23
for the denominator.

To express maximum and minimum, it clearly suffices to express the
maximum and minimum of a family of nonnegative dyadic rationals

\left(p_{i}\cdot 2^{-s_{i}}\right)_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}
for some definable finite
index set
{\mathcal{I}}
. Using Lemma
3.23
, we can determine

s\coloneqq\max_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}s_{i}
. Then we need to determine maximum
and minimum of the natural numbers
q_{i}\coloneqq p_{i}2^{s-s_{i}}
, which we can do by applying Lemma
3.27
.

For division, the situation is slightly more complicated, because
the dyadic rationals are not closed under division. We only get an
approximation. We use a
0
-ary function variable to control the
additive approximation error.

Lemma 3.35
.

Let
\boldsymbol{Z}_{1},\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}
be r-schemas of type
{\emptyset\to\texttt{r}}
, and
let
W
be a function variable of type
\emptyset\to\texttt{n}
. Then
there is an arithmetical r-expression
div

such
that for all structures
A
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
, if

\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}\neq 0
then

\left|\frac{\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}}{\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{2}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}}-\left\llangle\textsf{div}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}\right|<2^{-\mathcal{a}(W)}.

Proof 3.36
.

This follows easily from Lemma
3.12
.

3.6
Evaluating Feedforward Neural Networks

The most important consequence the results of the previous section
have for us is that we can simulate rational piecewise-linear
FNNs.

Let us first see how we deal with the activation functions. To
represent a rational piecewise linear function, we need an integer
k

as well as three families of dyadic rationals: the thresholds

(t_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}
, the slopes
(a_{i})_{0\leq i\leq k}
and the
constant terms
(b_{i})_{0\leq i\leq k}
. An
L-schema
of type

{\boldsymbol{t}\to\texttt{L}}
, for some
\boldsymbol{t}\in\{\texttt{v},\texttt{n}\}^{k}
, is a tuple

\boldsymbol{Z}=(Z_{\textup{len}},\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{th}},\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{sl}},\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{co}})
, where
Z_{\textup{len}}
is a function
variable of type
\boldsymbol{t}\to\texttt{n}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{th}},\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{sl}},\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{co}}
are
r-schemas of type
\texttt{n}\boldsymbol{t}
. Let
A
be a structure,
\mathcal{a}
an
assignment over
A
, and
\boldsymbol{c}\in A^{\boldsymbol{t}}
. Let

k\coloneqq\mathcal{a}(Z_{\textup{len}})(\boldsymbol{c})
. For
1\leq i\leq k
,
let

t_{i}\coloneqq\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{th}}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(i,\boldsymbol{c})
. For
0\leq i\leq k
, let

a_{i}\coloneqq\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{sl}}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(i,\boldsymbol{c})
and

b_{i}\coloneqq\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{co}}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(i,\boldsymbol{c})
. Then if

t_{1}<\ldots<t_{k}
and for all
i\in[k]
we have

a_{i-1}t_{i}+b_{i-1}=a_{i}t_{i}+b_{i}
, we define
\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
to
be the rational piecewise linear function with thresholds
t_{i}
, slopes

a_{i}
, and constants
b_{i}
. The condition

a_{i-1}t_{i}+b_{i-1}=a_{i}t_{i}+b_{i}
guarantees that this function is
continuous. Otherwise, we define
\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
to be identically
0
. We can also
define
L-expressions
consisting of formulas of the appropriate
types.

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
\boldsymbol{Y}
be an L-schema of type
{\emptyset\to\texttt{L}}
, and let
\boldsymbol{Z}

be an r-schema of type
{\emptyset\to\texttt{r}}
. Then there is an
arithmetical r-expression

apply
such that for all numerical assignments

\mathcal{a}
,

\left\llangle\textsf{apply}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}=\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Y}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}\Big(\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}\Big).

Proof 3.38
.

This follows easily from Lemma
3.31
.

To represent an FNN we need to represent the skeleton as well as
all activation functions and parameters. An
F-schema
of type

{\boldsymbol{t}\to\texttt{F}}
for some
\boldsymbol{t}\in\{\texttt{v},\texttt{n}\}^{k}
is a
tuple
\boldsymbol{Z}=(Z_{\textup{V}},Z_{\textup{E}},\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{ac}},\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{wt}},\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{bi}})
where
Z_{\textup{V}}
is a function
variable
\boldsymbol{t}\to\texttt{n}
,
Z_{\textup{E}}
is a relation
variable of type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{n}^{2}\boldsymbol{t}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
,
\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{ac}}
is an L-schema of
type
{\texttt{n}\boldsymbol{t}\to\texttt{L}}
,
\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{wt}}
is an r-schema of
type
{\texttt{n}^{2}\boldsymbol{t}\to\texttt{r}}
, and
\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{bi}}
is an r-schema of
type
{\texttt{n}\boldsymbol{t}\to\texttt{r}}
. Then for every structure
A
, every
assignment
\mathcal{a}
over
A
, and every tuple
\boldsymbol{c}\in A^{\boldsymbol{t}}
, we
define
(V,E,({\mathfrak{a}}_{v})_{v\in V},(w_{e})_{e\in E},(b_{v})_{v\in V})
as follows:

•

V\coloneqq\{0,\ldots,\mathcal{a}(Z_{\textup{V}})(\boldsymbol{c})\}
;

•

E\coloneqq\{ij\in V^{2}\mid ij\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathcal{a}(Z_{\textup{E}})\}
;

•

{\mathfrak{a}}_{i}=\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{ac}}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(i,\boldsymbol{c})
for
i\in V
;

•

w_{ij}=\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{wt}}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(i,j,\boldsymbol{c})
for
ij\in E
;

•

b_{i}=\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}_{\textup{bi}}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(i,\boldsymbol{c})
for
i\in V
.

Then if
(V,E)
is a dag,
\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}\coloneqq(V,E,({\mathfrak{a}}_{v})_{v\in V},(w_{e})_{e\in E},(b_{v})_{v\in V})
is an FNN. The input nodes
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}
of this FNN are the sources of the
dag
(V,E)
in their natural order (as natural numbers). Similarly,
the output nodes
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}
of the FNN are the sinks of the
dag
(V,E)
in their natural order. If
(V,E)
is not a
dag, we simply define
\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}
to be the trivial FNN with a
single node, which computes the identity function. We can also define

F-expressions
consisting of formulas of the appropriate types.

Lemma 3.39
.

Let
\boldsymbol{Z}
be an F-schema of type
{\emptyset\to\texttt{F}}
, and let

\boldsymbol{X}
be an r-schema of type
{\texttt{n}\to\texttt{r}}
. Then for every

t\geq 0
there is an arithmetical r-expression
\textsf{eval}_{t}(y)
such that the
following holds. Let
\mathcal{a}
be a numerical assignment
and
{\mathfrak{F}}\coloneqq\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}
. Suppose that the input
dimension of
{\mathfrak{F}}
is
p
, and let

\boldsymbol{x}\coloneqq\big(\left\llangle\boldsymbol{X}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}(0),\ldots,\left\llangle\boldsymbol{X}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}(p-1)\big).

Then for every node
v
of
{\mathfrak{F}}
of depth
t
it holds that

f_{{\mathfrak{F}},v}(\boldsymbol{x})=\left\llangle\textsf{eval}_{t}\right\rrangle^{\mathcal{a}}(v).

Proof 3.40
.

Using the formulas for multiplication and iterated addition, it easy
to construct
\textsf{eval}_{t}
by induction on
t
.

Corollary 3.41
.

Let
\boldsymbol{Z}
be an F-schema of type
{\emptyset\to\texttt{F}}
, and let

\boldsymbol{X}
be r-schemas of type
{\texttt{n}\to\texttt{r}}
. Then for every
d>0
there is an arithmetical r-expression
\textsf{eval}_{d}(y)
such that the
following holds. Let
A
be a structure,
\mathcal{a}
an assignment,
and
{\mathfrak{F}}\coloneqq\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}
. Suppose that the
depth of
(V,E)
is at most
d
, and let
p
be the input dimension
and
q
the output dimension. Let

\boldsymbol{x}\coloneqq\big(\left\llangle\boldsymbol{X}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(0),\ldots,\left\llangle\boldsymbol{X}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(p-1)\big).

Then

{\mathfrak{F}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\big(\left\llangle\textsf{eval}_{d}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(0),\ldots,\left\llangle\textsf{eval}_{d}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(q-1)\big).

Corollary 3.42
.

Let
{\mathfrak{F}}
be a rational piecewise linear FNN of input dimension
p

and output dimension
q
, and let
\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{X}_{p}
be r-schemas of type

{\emptyset\to\texttt{r}}
. Then for all
i\in[q]
there is an
arithmetical r-expression

\textsf{eval}_{{\mathfrak{F}},i}
such that for all structures
A
and
assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
,

{\mathfrak{F}}\Big(\left\llangle\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})},\ldots,\left\llangle\boldsymbol{X}_{p}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}\Big)=\Big(\left\llangle\textsf{eval}_{{\mathfrak{F}},1}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})},\ldots,\left\llangle\textsf{eval}_{{\mathfrak{F}},q}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}\Big).

3.7
Fragments of
FO+C

To describe the expressiveness of graph neural networks, we need to
consider various fragments of
FO+C
. For
k\geq 1
, the

k
-variable fragment

FO
k
+C
of
FO+C
consists of all
formulas with at most
k
vertex variables. Importantly, the number of
number variables is unrestricted. We call an
FO
k
+C
-formula

decomposable
if it contains no relation variables or function
variables and every subformula with exactly
k
free vertex variables
is a Boolean combination of relational atoms and formulas with at most

k-1
free vertex variables. Equivalently, an
FO
k
+C
-formula is
decomposable if it contains no relation variables or function
variables and every subformula of the form
\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}
, for
terms
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
, has at most
k-1
free vertex variables. Note
that this implies that a decomposable
FO
k
+C
-formula contains no
terms with
k
free vertex variables.

Example 3.43
.

The
FO
2
+C
-formula

\varphi(z)\coloneqq\exists x_{1}.\exists x_{2}.\big(E(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge z=\#x_{2}.E(x_{2},x_{1})\wedge z=\#x_{1}.E(x_{2},x_{1})\big)

is decomposable, whereas the
FO
2
+C
-formula

\psi(z)\coloneqq\exists x_{1}.\exists x_{2}.\Big(E(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge z=\big(\#x_{2}.E(x_{2},x_{1})\big)\cdot\big(\#x_{1}.E(x_{2},x_{1})\big)\Big)

is not. However,
\psi(z)
is decomposable if viewed as an

FO
3
+C
-formula.

Lemma 3.44
.

Let
\varphi
be an
FO+C
-formula of vocabulary
\tau\cup\{\leqslant\}

with at most one free vertex variable and no relation or function variables. Then there is a decomposable
FO
2
+C
-formula

\varphi^{\prime}
such that for all ordered
\tau
-structures
A
and all
assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
it holds that

A\models\varphi\iff A\models\varphi^{\prime}
.

Proof 3.45
.

We first define a bijection between the vertices of the structure

A
and an initial segment of
{\mathbb{N}}
. We simply let

\textsf{bij}(x,y)\coloneqq\#x^{\prime}.x^{\prime}\leqslant x=\#(y^{\prime}\leq\textsf{ord}).y^{\prime}\leq y
. We introduce a distinguished number variable
y_{x}
for
every vertex variable
x
.

To obtain
\varphi^{\prime}
from
\varphi
, we first replace quantification over

x
in counting terms by quantification over
y_{x}
, that is, we
replace
\#(x,\ldots)
by
\#(y_{x}<\textsf{ord},\ldots)
. Furthermore, we
replace atomic formulas
x=x^{\prime}
by
y_{x}=y_{x^{\prime}}
(or, more formally,

y_{x}\leq y_{x^{\prime}}\wedge y_{x^{\prime}}\leq y_{x}
) and atomic formulas
R(x,x^{\prime})
by

\exists x_{1}.\exists x_{2}.\big(\textsf{bij}(x_{1},y_{x})\wedge\textsf{bij}(x_{2},y_{x^{\prime}})\wedge R(x_{1},x_{2}))
. Let
\psi
be the resulting formula. If
\varphi
has no
free variables, we let
\varphi^{\prime}\coloneqq\psi
. If
\varphi
has one free
variable
x
, we let

\varphi^{\prime}\coloneqq\exists y_{x}<\textsf{ord}.\big(\textsf{bij}(x,y_{x})\wedge\psi\big).

Then
\varphi^{\prime}
is
equivalent to
\varphi
, and it only contains the vertex variables

x_{1},x_{2}
and hence is in
FO
2
+C
. It is easy to check that the
formula is decomposable.

Remark 3.46
.

Note that Lemma
3.44
implies that on ordered structures, every

FO
2
+C
-formula with at most one free variable is equivalent to a decomposable formula. It is an
open problem whether this holds on arbitrary structures.

The definition of decomposable
FO
k
+C
is not
particularly intuitive, at least at first glance. However, we wonder
if “decomposable
FO
k
+C
” is what we actually want as the

k
-variable fragment of
FO+C
. This view is not only supported by
Lemma
3.44
, but also by the observation that the logic

\textsf{C}^{k}
(the
k
-variable fragment of the extension of first-order
logic by counting quantifiers
\exists^{\geq n}x
) is contained in
decomposable
FO
k
+C
. Furthermore, characterisations of

k
-variable logics in terms of pebble games or the WL-algorithm
only take atomic properties of
k
-tuples into account.

The
guarded fragment
GFO+C
is a fragment of
FO
2
+C
where
quantification and counting is restricted to range over neighbours of
a free variable. We fix two variables
x_{1},x_{2}
. A
guard
is an
atomic formula of the form
R(x_{i},x_{3-i})
for some binary relation
symbol
R
. We inductively define the sets of
GFO+C
-terms
and

GFO+C
-formulas
as follows.

•

All number variables and
\textsf{0},\textsf{1},\textsf{ord}
are
GFO+C
-terms.

•

For all
GFO+C
-terms
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
, the expressions

\theta+\theta^{\prime}
and
\theta\cdot\theta^{\prime}

are
GFO+C
-terms.

•

For all function variables
U
of type
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\to\texttt{n}

and all tuples
(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})
, where
\xi_{i}
is a vertex
variable if
t_{i}=\texttt{v}
and
\xi_{i}
is a
GFO+C
-term if
t_{i}=\texttt{n}
,
the expression

U(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})
is a
GFO+C
-term.

•

For all
GFO+C
-terms
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
, the expression
\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}

is a
GFO+C
-formula.

•

All relational atoms whose variables are among
x_{1},x_{2}
are

GFO+C
-formulas.

•

For all relation variables
X
of type

\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
and all tuples

(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})
, where
\xi_{i}
is a vertex
variable if
t_{i}=\texttt{v}
and
\xi_{i}
is a
GFO+C
-term if
t_{i}=\texttt{n}
,
the expression

X(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})
is a
GFO+C
-formula.

•

For all
GFO+C
-formulas
\varphi,\psi
the expressions
\neg\varphi

and
\varphi\wedge\psi

are
GFO+C
-formulas.

•

For all
GFO+C
-formulas
\varphi
, guards

\gamma
, number variables

y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
, all
GFO+C
-terms
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}
, and

i=1,2
,

\#(x_{3-i},y_{1}<\theta_{1},\ldots,y_{k}<\theta_{k}).(\gamma\wedge\varphi),

(14)

is a
GFO+C
-term.

•

For all
GFO+C
-formulas
\varphi
, number variables

y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
, and
GFO+C
-terms
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}
,

\#(y_{1}<\theta_{1},\ldots,y_{k}<\theta_{k}).\varphi,

(15)

is a
GFO+C
-term.

Observe that a
GFO+C
-term or
GFO+C
-formula either has at least one
free vertex variable or contains no vertex variable at
all. Note that
we add
ord
as a “built-in” constant that is always interpreted by
the order of the input structure. We need access to the order of a
structure to bound quantification on numbers, and the closed

FO
2
+C
-term
\textsf{ord}=\#x.x=x
defining the order is not in

GFO+C
.

An r-expression is
guarded
if all its formulas and terms are in

GFO+C
.

Remark 3.47
.

Our definition of the guarded fragment is relatively liberal in
terms of which kind of formulas
\varphi
we allow inside the
guarded counting operators in (
14
). In particular, we allow
both
x_{i}
and
x_{3-i}
to occur freely in
\varphi
. A more
restrictive alternative
definition, more in the spirit of a modal logic, would be to stipulate
that the variable
x_{i}
must not occur freely in
\varphi
and the
bounding terms
\theta_{i}
. Let us call
the restriction of
GFO+C
where terms of the form (
14
) are
only allowed for formulas
\varphi
and terms
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}
in which
x_{i}
does not occur
freely the
modal fragment
of
FO+C
, denoted by
MFO+C
.

While our main focus will be on
GFO+C
, we will explain how to adapt
our main results to
MFO+C
in a sequence of remarks. The distinction
between the modal and guarded fragments is closely related to a
similar distinction for graph
neural networks (see Remark
4.1
).

It is proved in the subsequent article
[
12
]
that
GFO+C
and

MFO+C
have the same expressive power. The characterisation theorems
of this article will be used there to transfer this to graph neural
networks.

By definition,
GFO+C
is contained in
FO
2
+C
. The converse does not
hold. Let us introduce an intermediate fragment
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
which extends

GFO+C
and is still in
FO
2
+C
. We call
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
the
guarded
fragment with global counting
. In addition to the guarded counting
terms in (
14
), in
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
formulas we also allow a restricted
form of unguarded counting in the form

\#(x_{3-i},y_{1}<\theta_{1},\ldots,y_{k}<\theta_{k}).\varphi,

(16)

where the variable
x_{i}
must not occur freely in

\varphi
. Intuitively, such a term makes a “global” calculation that
is unrelated to the “local” properties of the free
variable
x_{i}
.

Let us call a
GFO+C
-formula or a
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-formula

decomposable
if it is decomposable as an
FO
2
+C
-formula.

Lemma 3.48
.

For every decomposable
FO
2
+C
-formula
\varphi
there is
a decomposable
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-formula
\varphi^{\prime}
such that for all graphs
G
, possibly
labelled, and all assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
G
we have

(G,\mathcal{a})\models\varphi\iff(G,\mathcal{a})\models\varphi^{\prime}.

Proof 3.49
.

Throughout this proof, formulas are without relation or function
variables. We write
x
and
x^{\prime}
to refer to the variables

x_{1},x_{2}
, with the understanding that if
x
refers to
x_{i}
then

x^{\prime}
refers to
x_{3-i}
. We need to replace unguarded terms by
combinations of terms of the form (
14
), (
15
), and
(
16
).

{techclaim}

Every term
\eta\coloneqq\#(x^{\prime},y_{1}<\theta_{1},\ldots,y_{k}<\theta_{k}).\psi
, where

\psi
is a
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-formula and the
\theta_{i}
are
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-terms,
is equivalent to a
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-term.

{subproof}

By Lemma
3.2
, we may assume without loss of
generality that the terms
\theta_{i}
do not contain the variables

x,x^{\prime},y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
(and therefore the counting operator is
nonadaptive). Indeed, by the lemma we can find a term
\theta

built from

ord
and the free number variables of
\eta

such that
\theta
bounds all
\theta_{i}
. Then
\eta
is equivalent to

\#(x^{\prime},y_{1}<\theta,\ldots,y_{k}<\theta).(\psi\wedge y_{1}<\theta_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge y_{k}<\theta_{k})
.

Also without loss of generality we may assume that
k\geq 1
,
because we can always append
y<\textsf{1}
for a fresh variable
y
in
the counting operator without changing the result. To simplify the
notation, let us assume that
k=1
. The generalisation to larger
k

is straightforward. Hence

\eta=\#(x^{\prime},y<\theta).\psi\\

(17)

and
\theta
is a term in which neither
x
nor
x^{\prime}
is free.

Since
\psi
is decomposable, we can re-write
\psi
as

(E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\psi_{1})\vee(\neg E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\neg x=x^{\prime}\wedge\psi_{2})\vee\psi_{3},

where
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are Boolean combinations of formulas
with at most one free vertex variable (either
x
or
x^{\prime}
), and
x^{\prime}

does not occur freely in
\psi_{3}
. To see this, note that in graphs
all vertices
x,x^{\prime}

satisfy exactly one of
E(x,x^{\prime})
,
\neg E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\neg x=x^{\prime}
, and

x=x^{\prime}
. Thus
\psi
is equivalent to the disjunction

(E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\psi)\vee(\neg E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\neg x=x^{\prime}\wedge\psi)\vee(x=x^{\prime}\wedge\psi).

Then to obtain
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
, we eliminate all atomic formulas

E(x,x^{\prime}),x=x^{\prime}
in both free variables from
\psi
, and to obtain

\psi_{3}
we substitute
x
for all free occurrence of
x^{\prime}
. Thus

\eta
is equivalent to the term

\displaystyle\#(x^{\prime},y<\theta).(E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\psi_{1})

\displaystyle+\;

\displaystyle\#(x^{\prime},y<\theta).(\neg E^{\prime}(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\neg x=x^{\prime}\wedge\psi_{2})

(18)

\displaystyle+\;

\displaystyle\#(y<\theta).\psi_{3}.

The first and the third term in this sum are already
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-terms
of the forms (
14
) and (
15
), respectively. We only
need to worry about the second,

\eta_{2}\coloneqq\#(x^{\prime},y<\theta).(\neg E^{\prime}(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\neg x=x^{\prime}\wedge\psi_{2}).

We can equivalently re-write
\eta_{2}
as

\displaystyle\#(x^{\prime},y<\theta).\psi_{2}

\displaystyle\dotminus\;

\displaystyle\#(x^{\prime},y<\theta).(E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\psi_{2})

\displaystyle\dotminus\;

\displaystyle\#(y<\theta).\psi_{2}\textstyle\frac{x}{x^{\prime}},

where
\psi_{2}\frac{x}{x^{\prime}}
denotes the formula obtained from
\psi_{2}
by
replacing all free occurrences of
x^{\prime}
by
x
. The second and the third term are already
GFO+C
-terms. We only
need to worry about the first,

\eta_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq\#(x^{\prime},y<\theta).\psi_{2}

Recall
that
\psi_{2}
is a Boolean combination of formulas with only one
free vertex variable. Bringing this Boolean combination into
disjunctive normal form, we obtain an equivalent formula

\bigvee_{i\in I}(\chi_{i}\wedge\chi^{\prime}_{i})
, where
x
does not
occur freely in
\chi^{\prime}_{i}
and
x^{\prime}
does not
occur freely in
\chi_{i}
. We can further ensure that the disjuncts
are mutually exclusive, that is, for every pair
x,x^{\prime}
there is at
most one
i
such that that it satisfies

(\chi_{i}\wedge\chi^{\prime}_{i})
. For example, if
I=\{1,2\}
, we
note that

(\chi_{1}\wedge\chi^{\prime}_{1})\vee(\chi_{2}\wedge\chi^{\prime}_{2})
is
equivalent to

\displaystyle((\chi_{1}\wedge\chi_{2})\wedge(\chi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\chi_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\vee

\displaystyle((\chi_{1}\wedge\neg\chi_{2})\wedge(\chi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\chi_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\vee

\displaystyle((\chi_{1}\wedge\chi_{2})\wedge(\chi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\neg\chi_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\vee

\displaystyle((\chi_{1}\wedge\neg\chi_{2})\wedge(\chi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\neg\chi_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\vee

\displaystyle((\neg\chi_{1}\wedge\chi_{2})\wedge(\chi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\chi_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\vee

\displaystyle((\chi_{1}\wedge\chi_{2})\wedge(\neg\chi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\chi_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\vee

\displaystyle((\neg\chi_{1}\wedge\chi_{2})\wedge(\neg\chi^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\chi_{2}^{\prime})).

Then
\eta_{2}^{\prime}
is equivalent to the term

\sum_{i\in I}\#(x^{\prime},y<\theta).(\chi_{i}\wedge\chi^{\prime}_{i}).

Consider a summand

\eta_{2,i}\coloneqq\#(x^{\prime},y<\theta).(\chi_{i}\wedge\chi^{\prime}_{i})
.
Let
\zeta\coloneqq\#x^{\prime}.(\chi_{i}\wedge\chi^{\prime}_{i})
and note that
for all graphs
G
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
we have

\left\lsem\eta_{2,i}\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})}=\sum_{b<\left\lsem\theta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})}}\left\lsem\zeta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a}\frac{b}{y})}=\big|\big\{(b,c)\mathrel{\big|}b<\left\lsem\theta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})},c<\left\lsem\zeta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a}\frac{b}{y})}\big\}\big|.

Let
\eta^{\prime}_{2,i}\coloneqq\#(y<\theta,z<\textsf{ord}).z<\zeta
. Since we
always have
\left\lsem\zeta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})}\leq|G|
, the terms
\eta_{2,i}
and

\eta_{2,i}^{\prime}
are equivalent.

The final step is to turn
\zeta
into
a
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-term. Recall that
\zeta=\#x^{\prime}.(\chi_{i}\wedge\chi^{\prime}_{i})

and that

x^{\prime}
is not free in
\chi_{i}
. If
x
does not satisfy
\chi_{i}
, then the term

\zeta
evaluates to
0
, and otherwise it has the same value as the
term
\#x^{\prime}.\chi^{\prime}_{i}
, which is of the form (
16
).
Note that the term

\#(y^{\prime}<\textsf{1}).\chi_{i}
, where
y^{\prime}
is a fresh number variable not
occurring in
\chi_{i}
, is of the form (
15
) and evaluates
to
1
if
\chi_{i}
is satisfied and to
0
otherwise. Thus the
term

\#(y^{\prime}<\textsf{1}).\chi_{i}\;\cdot\;\#x^{\prime}.\chi_{i}^{\prime}

is a
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-term equivalent to
\zeta
. This completes the
proof of the claim.

{techclaim}

Every term
\eta\coloneqq\#(x,x^{\prime},y_{1}<\theta_{1},\ldots,y_{k}<\theta_{k}).\psi
, where

\psi
is a
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-formula and the
\theta_{i}
are
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-terms,
is equivalent to a
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-term.

{subproof}

Arguing as in the proof of Claim
3.49
, we may assume that

\eta=\#(x,x^{\prime},y<\theta).\psi,

(19)

where
\theta
is a term in which the variables
x,x^{\prime}
are not free.

We proceed very similarly to the proof of Claim
3.49
. The first step is to rewrite the term as
the sum

\displaystyle\#(x,x^{\prime},y<\theta).(E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\psi_{1})

\displaystyle+\;

\displaystyle\#(x,x^{\prime},y<\theta).(\neg E^{\prime}(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\neg x=x^{\prime}\wedge\psi_{2})

(20)

\displaystyle+\;

\displaystyle\#(x,y<\theta).\psi_{3},

where
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are Boolean combinations of formulas
with at most one free vertex variable and
x^{\prime}
is not free in

\psi_{3}
. Then the third term is already of the form (
16
),
and we only have to deal with the first two.

Let us look at the term

\eta_{1}\coloneqq\#(x,x^{\prime},y<\theta).(E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\psi_{1})
. Let

\zeta_{1}\coloneqq\#(x^{\prime},y<\theta).(E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\psi_{1})
. Then

\zeta_{1}
is a term of the form (
14
). Moreover, for every
graph
G
and assignment
\mathcal{a}
we have

\displaystyle\left\lsem\eta_{1}\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in V(G)}\left\lsem\zeta_{1}\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a}\frac{a}{x})}=\big|\big\{(a,c)\mathrel{\big|}a\in V(G),c<\left\lsem\zeta_{1}\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a}\frac{a}{x})}\big\}\big|.

We let

\eta_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\#(x,z<\textsf{ord}).z<\zeta_{1}.

Then
\eta_{1}^{\prime}
is a term of the form (
16
) that is equivalent
to
\eta_{1}
.

It remains to deal with the second summand in (
20
), the
term

\eta_{2}\coloneqq\#(x,x^{\prime},y<\theta).(\neg E^{\prime}(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\neg x=x^{\prime}\wedge\psi_{2}).

We rewrite this term as

\displaystyle\#(x,x^{\prime},y<\theta).\psi_{2}

(21)

\displaystyle\dotminus\;

\displaystyle\#(x,x^{\prime},y<\theta).(E(x,x^{\prime})\wedge\psi_{2})

(22)

\displaystyle\dotminus\;

\displaystyle\#(x,y<\theta).\psi_{2}\textstyle\frac{x}{x^{\prime}}.

(23)

The term (
23
) is of the form (
16
), and we have
just seen how to deal with a term of the form (
22
). Thus
it remains to deal with the first term
\eta_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq\#(x,x^{\prime},y<\theta).\psi_{2}
. As in the proof of Claim
3.49
, we can find an equivalent formula

\bigvee_{i\in I}(\chi_{i}\wedge\chi^{\prime}_{i})
, where
x
does not
occur freely in
\chi^{\prime}_{i}
and
x^{\prime}
does not
occur freely in
\chi_{i}
, and the disjuncts
are mutually exclusive. Then
\eta_{2}^{\prime}
is equivalent to the sum

\sum_{i\in I}\#(x,x^{\prime},y<\theta).(\chi_{i}\wedge\chi_{i}^{\prime}).

Consider one of the terms in the sum,
\eta_{2,i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\#(x,x^{\prime},y<\theta).(\chi_{i}\wedge\chi_{i}^{\prime})
.
We let
\zeta\coloneqq\#(x,x^{\prime}).(\chi_{i}\wedge\chi^{\prime}_{i})
As in the proof of Claim
3.49
,
for all graphs
G
and assignments
\mathcal{a}
we have

\left\lsem\eta_{2,i}^{\prime}\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})}=\sum_{b<\left\lsem\theta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})}}\left\lsem\zeta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a}\frac{b}{y})}=\big|\big\{(b,c)\mathrel{\big|}b<\left\lsem\theta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})},c<\left\lsem\zeta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})}\big\}\big|.

Let
\eta^{\prime\prime}_{2,i}\coloneqq\#(y<\theta,z<\textsf{ord}\cdot\textsf{ord}).z<\zeta
. Since we
always have
\left\lsem\zeta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})}\leq|G|^{2}
, the terms
\eta_{2,i}^{\prime}
and

\eta_{2,i}^{\prime\prime}
are equivalent.

To turn
\zeta
into
a
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-term
\zeta^{\prime}
, we observe that for all graphs
G
and assignments

\mathcal{a}
we have

\left\lsem\zeta\right\rsem^{(G,\mathcal{a})}=\big|\big\{a\in V(G)\mathrel{\big|}(G,\mathcal{a}\textstyle\frac{a}{x})\models\chi_{i}\big\}\big|\cdot\big|\big\{a^{\prime}\in V(G)\mathrel{\big|}(G,\mathcal{a}\textstyle\frac{a^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}})\models\chi_{i}^{\prime}\big\}\big|.

We let
\zeta^{\prime}\coloneqq\#x.\chi_{i}\cdot\#x^{\prime}.\chi_{i}^{\prime}
.

With these two claims, it is easy to inductively translate a
decomposable
FO
2
+C
-formula into an
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}
-formula.

Combining Lemmas
3.48
and
3.44
with
Corollary
3.7
, we obtain the following.

Corollary 3.50
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}
be a unary query. Then
L_{\leqslant}({\mathcal{Q}})
is in
\textsf{TC}^{0}
if
and only if
{\mathcal{Q}}
is definable in order-invariant
\textsf{GFO+C}^{\textup{gc}}_{\textup{nu}}
.

3.8
Arithmetic in
GFO+C

Since all arithmetical
FO+C
-formulas and terms are in
GFO+C
, most results of
Sections
3.4
–
3.6
apply to

GFO+C
. Exceptions are Lemmas
3.23
,
3.25
, and

3.33
on iterated addition, which may involve vertex
variables. Here we prove variants of these lemmas for the guarded
fragment.

Lemma 3.51
.

Let
X
be a relation variable of type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{v}^{2}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
,
and let
U,V
be function variables of types

\texttt{v}^{2}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\to\texttt{n}
,
\texttt{v}^{2}\to\texttt{n}
, respectively.
Furthermore, let

\gamma(x,x^{\prime})
be a guard.

1.

There is a
GFO+C
-term
\textsf{u-itadd}(x)
such that for all
structures
A
, assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
, and
a\in V(A)
,

\left\lsem\textsf{u-itadd}\right\rsem^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(a)=\sum_{(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})}\mathcal{a}(U)(a,a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}),

where the sum ranges over all
(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})\in V(A)\times{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}

such that
A\models\gamma(a,a^{\prime})
and
(a,a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)
and
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with

b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(a,a^{\prime})
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

2.

There are
GFO+C
-terms
\textsf{u-max}(x)
and
\textsf{u-min}(x)
such that for
all structures
A
, assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
, and
a\in V(A)
,

\displaystyle\left\lsem\textsf{u-max}\right\rsem^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(a)

\displaystyle=\max_{(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})}\mathcal{a}(U)(a,a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}),

\displaystyle\left\lsem\textsf{u-min}\right\rsem^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(a)

\displaystyle=\min_{(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})}\mathcal{a}(U)(a,a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}),

where
\max
and
\min
range over all
(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})\in V(A)\times{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}

such that
A\models\gamma(a,a^{\prime})
and
(a,a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)
and
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with

b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(a,a^{\prime})
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

Proof 3.52
.

The proof is very similar to the proof of Lemma
3.23
, we
just have to make sure that the terms we define are guarded. Again,
we assume for simplicity that
\ell=1
.

We let

\textsf{u-itadd}(x)\coloneqq\#\big(x^{\prime},y<V(x,x^{\prime}),z<U(x,x^{\prime},y)\big).\big(\gamma(x,x^{\prime})\wedge X(x,x^{\prime},y)\big)

and

\displaystyle\textsf{u-max}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\#z<\textsf{u-itadd}(x).\exists(x^{\prime},y<V(x,x^{\prime})).\big(\gamma(x,x^{\prime})\wedge X(x,x^{\prime},y)\wedge z<U(x,x^{\prime})\big),

\displaystyle\textsf{u-min}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\#z<\textsf{u-itadd}(x).\forall(x^{\prime},y<V(x,x^{\prime})).\big(\gamma(x,x^{\prime})\wedge X(x,x^{\prime},y)\to z<U(x,x^{\prime})\big).

Lemma 3.53
.

Let
\boldsymbol{Z}
be an r-schema of type
{\texttt{v}^{2}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\to\texttt{r}}
.
Let
X
be a relation variable of type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{v}^{2}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
,
and let
V
be a function variable of type

\texttt{v}^{2}\to\texttt{n}
. Furthermore, let

\gamma(x,x^{\prime})
be a guard.

1.

There is a guarded r-expression
\textsf{itadd}(x)
such that for all structures
A
, assignments
\mathcal{a}

over
A
, and
a\in V(A)
we have

\left\llangle\textsf{itadd}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(a)=\sum_{(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})}\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(a,a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}),

where the sum ranges over all
(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})\in V(A)\times{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}

such that
A\models\gamma(a,a^{\prime})
and
(a,a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)
and

\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with
b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(a,a^{\prime})

for all
i\in[\ell]
.

2.

There are guarded r-expressions
\textsf{max}(x)
and
\textsf{min}(x)
such that for all structures
A
, assignments
\mathcal{a}

over
A
, and
a\in V(A)
we have

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{max}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(a)

\displaystyle=\max_{(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})}\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(a,a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}),

\displaystyle\left\llangle\textsf{min}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(a)

\displaystyle=\min_{(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})}\left\llangle\boldsymbol{Z}\right\rrangle^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(a,a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}),

where
\max
and
\min
range over all
(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})\in V(A)\times{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}

such that
A\models\gamma(a,a^{\prime})
and
(a,a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)
and

\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with
b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(a,a^{\prime})

for all
i\in[\ell]
.

Proof 3.54
.

The proof is an easy adaptation of the proof of Lemma
3.33
,
arguing as in the proof of Lemma
3.51
to make sure that we
obtains guarded formulas and terms.

Remark 3.55
.

Lemmas
3.51
and
3.53
have analogues for the modal
fragment
MFO+C
. For Lemma
3.51
, we let
X
be a relation
variable of type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{v}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}

and
U,V
function variables of types

\texttt{v}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\to\texttt{n}
,
\texttt{v}\to\texttt{n}
, respectively. Then in the
assertions, we always omit
a
from the argument lists. For example,
statement (1) becomes:
There is an
MFO+C
-term
\textsf{u-itadd}(x)
such that for all
structures
A
, assignments
\mathcal{a}
over
A
, and
a\in V(A)
,

\left\lsem\textsf{u-itadd}\right\rsem^{(A,\mathcal{a})}(a)=\sum_{(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})}\mathcal{a}(U)(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}),

where the sum ranges over all
(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})\in V(A)\times{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}

such that
A\models\gamma(a,a^{\prime})
and
(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathcal{a}(X)
and
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\ell}
with

b_{i}<\mathcal{a}(V)(a^{\prime})
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

The modeification for assertion (2) is similar.

For Lemma
3.53
, we let
\boldsymbol{Z}
be an r-schema of type

{\texttt{v}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\to\texttt{r}}
,
X
a relation variable of type
\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{v}\texttt{n}^{\ell}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
,
and
V
be a function variable of type

\texttt{v}\to\texttt{n}
. The modification of the assertions is similar to the
previous lemma.

For both lemmas, the adaptation of the proof is straightforward.

4
Graph Neural Networks

We will work with standard message passing graph neural networks
(GNNs
7
7

7

We use the abbreviation GNN, but MPNN is also very common.
)

[
9
]
.
A GNN consists of a finite sequence of layers.
A
GNN layer
of input dimension
p
and output dimension
q
is
a triple
{\mathfrak{L}}=(\textsf{msg},\textsf{agg},\textsf{comb})
of functions: a
message function

\textsf{msg}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2p}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{p^{\prime}}
, an
aggregation function

agg
mapping finite multisets of vectors in
{\mathbb{R}}^{p^{\prime}}
to vectors
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{p^{\prime\prime}}
, and a
combination function

\textsf{comb}:{\mathbb{R}}^{p+p^{\prime\prime}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
.
A
GNN
is a tuple
{\mathfrak{N}}=({\mathfrak{L}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\mathfrak{L}}^{(d)})
of GNN layers, where the output dimension
q^{(i)}
of
{\mathfrak{L}}^{(i)}

matches the input dimension
p^{(i+1)}
of
{\mathfrak{L}}^{(i+1)}
. We call

q^{(0)}\coloneqq p^{(1)}
the input dimension of
{\mathfrak{N}}
and

q^{(d)}
the output dimension.

To define the semantics, let
{\mathfrak{L}}=(\textsf{msg},\textsf{agg},\textsf{comb})
be a GNN layer of input dimension
p

and output dimension
q
. It computes a function
{\mathfrak{L}}\colon{\mathcal{G}\!\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathcal{G}\!\mathcal{S}}_{q}
(as for circuits and feedforward neural networks, we
use the same letter to denote the network and the function it
computes) defined by
{\mathfrak{L}}(G,\mathcal{x})\coloneqq(G,\mathcal{y})
, where

\mathcal{y}:V(G)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
is defined by

\mathcal{y}(v)\coloneqq\textsf{comb}\Bigg(\mathcal{x}(v),\textsf{agg}\Big({\Big\{\hskip-4.5pt\Big\{}\textsf{msg}\big(\mathcal{x}(v),\mathcal{x}(w)\big)\mathrel{\Big|}w\in N_{G}(v){\Big\}\hskip-4.5pt\Big\}}\Big)\Bigg).

(24)

A GNN
{\mathfrak{N}}=({\mathfrak{L}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\mathfrak{L}}^{(d)})
composes the transformations
computed by its layers
{\mathfrak{L}}^{(i)}
, that is, it computes the function

{\mathfrak{N}}\colon{\mathcal{G}\!\mathcal{S}}_{q^{(0)}}\to{\mathcal{G}\!\mathcal{S}}_{q^{(d)}}
defined by

{\mathfrak{N}}(G,\mathcal{x})\coloneqq{\mathfrak{L}}^{(d)}\circ{\mathfrak{L}}^{(d-1)}\circ\ldots\circ{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1)}.

It will be convenient to also define
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{N}}}
as the function
mapping
(G,\mathcal{x})
to the signal
\mathcal{x}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{q^{(d)}}(G)
such that

{\mathfrak{N}}(G,\mathcal{x})=(G,\mathcal{x}^{\prime})
, so
{\mathfrak{N}}(G,\mathcal{x})=(G,\widetilde{\mathfrak{N}}(G,\mathcal{x}))
, and
similarly
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{L}}}
for a single layer
{\mathfrak{L}}
.

Remark 4.1
.

Our version of GNNs corresponds to the
message passing neural
networks
due to
[
9
]
. In
[
12
]
, these
GNNs are called
2-GNNs
, to distinguish them from another
common version of message passing graph neural networks, cleanly
formalised as the
aggregate-combine GNNs
in

[
3
]
, and called
1-GNNs
in

[
12
]
. In these 1-GNNs, messages
only depend on the vertex they are sent from, so the update rule
(
24
) becomes

\mathcal{y}(v)\coloneqq\textsf{comb}\Bigg(\mathcal{x}(v),\textsf{agg}\Big({\Big\{\hskip-4.5pt\Big\{}\textsf{msg}\big(\mathcal{x}(w)\big)\mathrel{\Big|}w\in N_{G}(v){\Big\}\hskip-4.5pt\Big\}}\Big)\Bigg).

(25)

Whenever we want to make the distinction between the two versions
explicit, we use the 1-GNN / 2-GNN terminology, but most of the time
we will just work with 2-GNNs and simply call them GNNs. The reason
I decided to focus on (2-)GNNs is that in practical work we also
found it beneficial to use them.

However, we will explain how to adapt our results to 1-GNNs in a
series of remarks leading to Theorem
7.9
. On the logical side, 2-GNNs correspond to the
guarded fragment
GFO+C
and
1
-GNNs to the modal fragement
MFO+C
of
first-order logic with counting
(see Remark
3.47
).

The relation between 1-GNNs
and 2-GNNs is more complicated than one might think; we refer the
reader to
[
12
]
.

So far, we have defined GNNs as an abstract computation model
computing transformations between graph signals. To turn them into
deep learning models, we represent the functions that specify the
layers by feedforward neural networks. More precisely, we assume that
the message functions
msg
and the combination functions
comb
of
all GNN layers are specified by FNNs
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\textsf{msg}}
and

{\mathfrak{F}}_{\textsf{comb}}
. Furthermore, we assume that the aggregation function

agg
is summation
SUM
, arithmetic mean
MEAN
, or
maximum
MAX
. Note that this means that the aggregation function
does not change the dimension, that is, we always have
p^{\prime}=p^{\prime\prime}

(referring to the description of GNN layers above).
To be able to deal with isolated nodes as well, we
define
\textsf{SUM}(\emptyset)\coloneqq\textsf{MEAN}(\emptyset)\coloneqq\textsf{MAX}(\emptyset)\coloneqq\boldsymbol{0}
.

If the FNNs
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\textsf{msg}}
and
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\textsf{comb}}
on all layers are (rational)
piecewise linear, we call the GNN
(rational) piecewise
linear
. Similarly, if they are rpl-approximable, we call the GNN

rpl-approximable
.

We mention a few extensions of the basic GNN model. Most importantly,
in a GNN with
global readout

[
3
]
(or,
equivalently, a GNN with a
virtual node

[
9
]
) in
each round the nodes also obtain the aggregation of the states of all
nodes in addition to the messages they receive from their
neighbours. So the state update rule (
24
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{y}(v)\coloneqq\textsf{comb}\bigg(\mathcal{x}(v),

\displaystyle\,\textsf{agg}\Big({\Big\{\hskip-4.5pt\Big\{}\textsf{msg}\big(\mathcal{x}(v),\mathcal{x}(w)\big)\mathrel{\Big|}w\in N_{G}(v){\Big\}\hskip-4.5pt\Big\}}\Big),

\displaystyle\,\textsf{agg}^{\prime}\Big({\Big\{\hskip-4.5pt\Big\{}\mathcal{x}(w)\mathrel{\Big|}w\in V(G){\Big\}\hskip-4.5pt\Big\}}\Big)\bigg).

We could also apply some function
\textsf{msg}^{\prime}
to the
\mathcal{x}(w)
before
aggregating, but this would not change the expressiveness, because we
can integrate this into the combination function.

To adapt GNNs to directed graphs, it is easiest to use separate
message functions for in-neighbours and out-neighbours and to
aggregate them separately and then combine both in the combination
function. In graphs with edge labels, or
edge signals

\mathcal{y}\colon E(G)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
for
some
k
, we can give these signals as a third
argument to the message function, so the message function becomes

\textsf{msg}\big(\mathcal{x}(v),\mathcal{x}(w),\mathcal{y}(v,w)\big)
. We can also adapt this to directed
edge-labeled graphs and hence to arbitrary binary relational
structures.
Often, we want to use GNNs to compute
graph-level
functions

{\mathcal{G}\!\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
rather than
node-level
functions

{\mathcal{G}\!\mathcal{S}}_{p}\to{\mathcal{G}\!\mathcal{S}}_{q}
. For this, we aggregate the values of the output
signal at the nodes to a single value. A
graph-level GNN

is a triple
{\mathfrak{G}}=({\mathfrak{N}},\textsf{agg},\textsf{ro})
consisting of a GNN
{\mathfrak{N}}
, say with input dimension
p
and output
dimension
p^{\prime}
, an
aggregate function

agg
, which we assume
to be either
SUM
or
MEAN
or
MAX
, and a

readout function

\textsf{ro}:{\mathbb{R}}^{p^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
, which we assume to
be computed by an FNN
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\textsf{ro}}
.

All of our results have straightforward extensions to all these
variants of the basic model.
Since the article is lengthy and
technical as it is, I decided to focus just on the basic model
here. Occasionally, I comment on some of the extensions, pointing
out which modifications on the logical side need to be made.

4.1
Useful Bounds

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}
be a GNN layer of input dimension
p
. Then there is a

\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
such that for
all graphs
G
, all signals
\mathcal{x}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}(G)
, and all vertices
v\in V(G)
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{L}}(G,\mathcal{x})(v)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\cdot\Big(\left\|\mathcal{x}|_{N[v]}\right\|_{\infty}+1\Big)\max\{\deg(v),1\}

(26)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\cdot\big(\left\|\mathcal{x}\right\|_{\infty}+1\big)|G|.

(27)

Recall that
\mathcal{x}|_{N[v]}
denotes the restriction of a signal

\mathcal{x}
to the closed neighbourhood
N[v]
of
v
, and we have

\left\|\mathcal{x}|_{N[v]}\right\|_{\infty}=\max_{w\in N[v]}\left\|\mathcal{x}(w)\right\|_{\infty}
.
The bound (
26
) is
local
, it only depends on the
neighbourhood of
v
. The
global
bound
(
27
) is simpler, but a bit weaker.

Proof 4.3
.

Clearly, (
26
) implies (
27
), so we only have to
prove the local bound (
26
). Let
msg
,
agg
,
comb
be the
message, aggregation, and combination functions of
{\mathfrak{L}}
. Let

{\mathfrak{F}}_{\textsf{msg}}
and
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\textsf{comb}}
be FNNs computing
msg
and
comb
,
respectively, and let
\gamma_{\textsf{msg}}\coloneqq\gamma({\mathfrak{F}}_{\textsf{msg}})
and

\gamma_{\textsf{comb}}\coloneqq\gamma({\mathfrak{F}}_{\textsf{comb}})
be the constants of
Lemma
2.6
(2).

Let
G
be a graph,
\mathcal{x}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}(G)
, and
v\in V(G)
. Then for all
w\in N_{G}(v)
we have

\left\|\textsf{msg}\big(\mathcal{x}(v),\mathcal{x}(w)\big)\right\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma_{\textsf{msg}}\cdot\Big(\left\|\big(\mathcal{x}(v),\mathcal{x}(w)\big)\right\|_{\infty}+1\Big)\leq\gamma_{\textsf{msg}}\cdot\big(\left\|\mathcal{x}|_{N[v]}\right\|_{\infty}+1\big).

Since for every multiset
M
we have
\textsf{agg}(M)\leq|M|\cdot m
, where

m
is the the maximum absolute value of the entries of
M
, it
follows that

\mathcal{z}(v)\coloneqq\textsf{agg}\Big({\Big\{\hskip-4.5pt\Big\{}\textsf{msg}\cdot\big(\mathcal{x}(v),\mathcal{x}(w)\big)\mathrel{\Big|}w\in N^{G}(v){\Big\}\hskip-4.5pt\Big\}}\Big)\leq\gamma_{\textsf{msg}}\big(\left\|\mathcal{x}|_{N[v]}\right\|_{\infty}+1\big)\deg(v).

Since
\gamma_{\textsf{msg}}\geq 1
, this implies

\left\|\big(\mathcal{x}(v),\mathcal{z}(v)\big)\right\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma_{\textsf{msg}}\cdot\big(\left\|\mathcal{x}|_{N[v]}\right\|_{\infty}+1\big)\max\{\deg(v),1\}.

Hence

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{{\mathfrak{L}}}(G,\mathcal{x})(v)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|\textsf{comb}\Big(\big(\mathcal{x}(v),\mathcal{z}(v)\big)\Big)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{\textsf{comb}}\cdot\big(\left\|\big(\mathcal{x}(v),\mathcal{z}(v)\big)\right\|_{\infty}+1\big)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{\textsf{comb}}\cdot\big(\gamma_{\textsf{msg}}\big(\left\|\mathcal{x}|_{N[v]}\right\|_{\infty}+1\big)\max\{\deg(v),1\}+1\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma_{\textsf{comb}}\gamma_{\textsf{msg}}\cdot(\left\|\mathcal{x}|_{N[v]}\right\|_{\infty}+1)\max\{\deg(v),1\}.

We let
\gamma\coloneqq 2\gamma_{\textsf{comb}}\gamma_{\textsf{msg}}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}
be a GNN layer of input dimension
p
. Then there is a
\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
such that for all
graphs
G
, all signals
\mathcal{x},\mathcal{x}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}(G)
, and all vertices
